Applications of distance measure between dual hesitant fuzzy sets in medical diagnosis and weighted dual hesitant fuzzy sets in making decision
Authors: Salah Boulaaras, Ghada E. Mostafa, Rashid Jan, Ibrahim Mekawy
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
This paper introduces a novel distance measure for dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFS) and weighted dual hesitant fuzzy sets (WDHFS), with a rigorous proof of the triangular inequality to ensure its mathematical validity. The proposed measure extends the normalized Hamming, generalized, and Euclidean distance measures to dual hesitant fuzzy elements (DHFE), offering a broader framework for handling uncertainty in fuzzy environments. Additionally, the utilization of a score function is shown to simplify the computation of these distance measures. The practical relevance of the proposed measure is demonstrated through its application in medical diagnosis and decision-making processes. A comparative analysis between the newly introduced distance measure denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ, and an existing measure, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1$$\end{document}χ1 is performed to underscore the superiority and potential advantages of the new approach in real-world scenarios.

Introduction

Dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFS) are an effective mathematical framework for representing real-world uncertainty by incorporating both membership and non-membership degrees, thereby facilitating the integration of fuzzy data in decision-making processes. Distance measures are fundamental to system theory and have wide-ranging applications in domains such as artificial intelligence, decision-making, and image segmentation4. Su et al.12 extended the concept of distance measures to DHFS, further enhancing their utility in various fields. The foundational work of Zadeh22, who introduced fuzzy sets and membership degrees to model complex systems, has had a profound and lasting influence on the field of engineering. Torra and Narukawa10,11 recently generalized traditional fuzzy sets to allow for multiple membership degrees, thereby improving the precision of fuzzy information representation. This advancement generated significant interest in hesitant fuzzy sets (HFS), establishing it as a prominent area of research for many scholars. Building on this, Xia and Xu17, along with Xia et al.18, explored the mathematical structure of HFS and developed several robust aggregation operators specifically designed for hesitant fuzzy environments. Xu and Xia19 further extended the concepts of cross-entropy and entropy to HFS, yielding notable results. In subsequent work, Xu and Xia20,21 investigated distance, correlation, and similarity measures for HFS. Farhadinia3 contributed to the field by examining information measures for both HFS and interval-valued hesitant fuzzy sets. Li et al.6,7 introduced the concept of hesitancy degree and formulated a distance measure based on it. To expand the application of HFS theory, Zeng et al.23 proposed a novel distance measure and applied it to pattern recognition. Distance and similarity measures play a crucial role across various domains, including image segmentation, approximate reasoning, medical diagnosis, and pattern recognition.

Mathematical analysis in science and engineering provides the necessary foundation for solving real-world problems by offering both theoretical insights and practical tools across a wide range of disciplines24–26. From designing efficient systems to analyzing complex data, these mathematical techniques drive innovation and understanding in modern science and engineering27–29. Disease symptoms often involve some level of fuzziness, making the accurate and rapid recognition of diseases a critical issue in pattern recognition. To tackle this issue, researchers have incorporated different fuzzy systems into medical diagnostics. Emanuel et al.1,2 implemented interval Type-2 fuzzy theory in medical diagnostics, achieving promising results. Similarly, Molla et al.8 applied Pythagorean fuzzy theory within the preference ranking organization method to improve evaluation techniques in medical diagnosis. In real-world scenarios, dual hesitant fuzzy sets are highly effective for managing uncertainty, prompting scholars to extend distance measures to these sets. For example, Singh9 developed a distance measure for dual hesitant fuzzy sets and used it for ranking investment alternatives. Su et al.12 proposed distance and similarity measures for dual hesitant fuzzy sets, applying them to pattern recognition. However, some existing distance measures for dual hesitant fuzzy sets fail to satisfy the triangle inequality, highlighting the need to revisit and redefine these measures13.

In decision-making contexts, dual hesitant fuzzy sets may not fully convey all relevant information to the decision maker, which can limit their effectiveness14,15. This limitation is addressed through the introduction of weighted dual hesitant fuzzy sets (WDHFS) and elements, which incorporate additional information in the decision-making process. In16, the authors systematically examined operations between weighted dual hesitant fuzzy elements (WDHFE), analyzed their mathematical properties, and proposed two aggregation operators: the weighted dual hesitant fuzzy weighted geometric operator and the weighted dual hesitant fuzzy weighted averaging operator. These operators facilitate the combination of weighted dual hesitant fuzzy information. Furthermore, they introduced the concept of a feature vector for WDHFE, which encapsulates five key properties: the lower bound, upper bound, variance function, hesitancy degree, and average level function. Leveraging this feature vector, the authors developed a distance measure that preserves the inherent information of WDHFE, ensuring a more accurate representation of the underlying fuzzy data.

Dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFS) provide a flexible framework for incorporating diverse evaluation results from experts, making them particularly advantageous in medical diagnosis and decision-making processes. Motivated by the capacity of DHFS to effectively handle uncertainty, Wenyi Zeng et al.13 introduced several key properties of dual hesitant fuzzy elements (DHFE), including hesitancy degree, variance function, and average function. They proposed novel distance measures for DHFE, as well as weighted distance measures for dual hesitant fuzzy sets, wherein the weights are used to adjust the significance of various feature indices. Comparative analyses with existing distance measures demonstrated the enhanced ability of dual hesitant fuzzy sets to accurately represent and describe medical diagnoses, underscoring their potential for improving decision-making accuracy in uncertain environments.

This paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel distance measure for dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFS) and weighted dual hesitant fuzzy sets (WDHFS), which addresses key challenges in handling uncertainty in fuzzy decision-making environments. The proposed distance measure offers a mathematically rigorous approach, as evidenced by the proof of the triangular inequality, ensuring its validity for practical applications. By extending well-known distance measures, such as the normalized Hamming, generalized, and Euclidean distances, to dual hesitant fuzzy elements (DHFE), this work enhances the ability to capture and process uncertain information more effectively. This novel measure has immediate practical implications, particularly in medical diagnosis and decision-making processes, where accurate representation and analysis of uncertain or imprecise data are essential. The comparative analysis with existing measures further highlights the potential advantages of the new approach, showcasing its relevance in real-world scenarios.

This paper is structured as follows: Section 2: We present the fundamental ideas of dual hesitant fuzzy sets, including the lower bound, upper bound, variance function, average level function, and hesitancy degree of dual hesitant fuzzy elements. Section 3: This section displays the main results of the paper, including the definition of a new distance measure and proofs of its characteristics. Additionally, the applications of this new distance measure in decision-making and medical diagnosis are discussed in the main results.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\upsilon \ge 0$$\end{document}υ≥0 , \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\ge u$$\end{document}1≥u,

In the following, we will consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q=\{\zeta (y),b(y)\}$$\end{document}q={ζ(y),b(y)} to be the dual hesitant fuzzy element.

Definition 4

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q=\{\zeta (y),b(y)\}$$\end{document}q={ζ(y),b(y)} is a dual hesitant fuzzy element, the score function I of q is stated as:2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I(q)=\frac{1}{t(\zeta (y))} \sum _{\upsilon \in \zeta }\upsilon - \frac{1}{t(b(y))} \sum _{r \in b}r. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I(q)=1t(ζ(y))∑υ∈ζυ-1t(b(y))∑r∈br.Accuracy function a of q is given by:2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a(q)=\frac{1}{t(\zeta (y))} \sum _{\upsilon \in \zeta }\upsilon + \frac{1}{t(b(y))} \sum _{r \in b}r. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a(q)=1t(ζ(y))∑υ∈ζυ+1t(b(y))∑r∈br.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1(y),q_2(y)$$\end{document}q1(y),q2(y) are dual hesitant fuzzy elements then if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(q_1(y))\le I(q_2(y))$$\end{document}I(q1(y))≤I(q2(y)) then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1(y)\le q_2(y)$$\end{document}q1(y)≤q2(y); if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(q_1(y))=I(q_2(y))$$\end{document}I(q1(y))=I(q2(y)) then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1(y)= q_2(y)$$\end{document}q1(y)≤q2(y) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(q_1)\le a(q_2)$$\end{document}a(q1)≤a(q2).

Definition 5

Definition 6

Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} distance measure or metric between E, F, or between any two dual Hesitant fuzzy sets.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F=\{(y,\zeta _F(y),b_F(y)); y\in \textrm{Z}\}$$\end{document}F={(y,ζF(y),bF(y));y∈Z},

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (E,F)\le \chi (E,V)+\chi (V,F)$$\end{document}χ(E,F)≤χ(E,V)+χ(V,F).

Definition 7

Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q=\{\zeta ,b\}$$\end{document}q={ζ,b} then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q^+=\{q_{\zeta }^+,q_{b}^+\}=\{max\{\upsilon \}_{\upsilon \in \zeta },min\{u\}_{u\in b}\}$$\end{document}q+={qζ+,qb+}={max{υ}υ∈ζ,min{u}u∈b}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q^-=\{q_{\zeta }^-,q_{b}^-\}=\{min\{\upsilon \}_{\upsilon \in \zeta },max\{u\}_{u\in b}\}$$\end{document}q-={qζ-,qb-}={min{υ}υ∈ζ,max{u}u∈b} are called the upper bound and lower bound of q, respectively.

Theorem 1

Theorem 2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1,q_2$$\end{document}q1,q2 be two dual hesitant fuzzy elements then the weighted normalized generalized distance measure, weighted normalized Hamming distance measure and weighted normalized Euclidean distance measure holds the triangle inequality.

Proof

The proof of this theorem is similar to Theorem 1. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}□

The main results

Definition 12

We can define a distance function (metric) or distance measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Definition 13

If every characteristic of the dual hesitant fuzzy element has different weights in the real application then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (q_1,q_2)$$\end{document}χ(q1,q2) is defined as follows:3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (q_1, q_2)&= \frac{ \left( \beta _1 |q_{1\zeta }^+ - q_{2\zeta }^+| + \beta _2 |q_{1\zeta }^- - q_{2\zeta }^-| + \beta _3 |q_{1b}^+ - q_{2b}^+| + \beta _4 |q_{1b}^- - q_{2b}^-| + \beta _5 |g_{1\zeta } - g_{2\zeta }|\right. }{ 1 + \left( \beta _1 |q_{1\zeta }^+ - q_{2\zeta }^+| + \beta _2 |q_{1\zeta }^- - q_{2\zeta }^-| + \beta _3 |q_{1b}^+ - q_{2b}^+| + \beta _4 |q_{1b}^- - q_{2b}^-| + \beta _5 |g_{1\zeta } - g_{2\zeta }| \right. } \nonumber \\ &\quad \frac{\left. + \beta _6 |g_{1b} - g_{2b}| + \beta _7 |v_{1\zeta } - v_{2\zeta }| + \beta _8 |v_{1b} - v_{2b}| + \beta _9 |h_{1\zeta } - h_{2\zeta }| + \beta _{10} |h_{1b} - h_{2b}| \right) }{\left. + \beta _6 |g_{1b} - g_{2b}| + \beta _7 |v_{1\zeta } - v_{2\zeta }| + \beta _8 |v_{1b} - v_{2b}| + \beta _9 |h_{1\zeta } - h_{2\zeta }| + \beta _{10} |h_{1b} - h_{2b}| \right) }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(q1,q2)=β1|q1ζ+-q2ζ+|+β2|q1ζ--q2ζ-|+β3|q1b+-q2b+|+β4|q1b--q2b-|+β5|g1ζ-g2ζ|1+β1|q1ζ+-q2ζ+|+β2|q1ζ--q2ζ-|+β3|q1b+-q2b+|+β4|q1b--q2b-|+β5|g1ζ-g2ζ|+β6|g1b-g2b|+β7|v1ζ-v2ζ|+β8|v1b-v2b|+β9|h1ζ-h2ζ|+β10|h1b-h2b|+β6|g1b-g2b|+β7|v1ζ-v2ζ|+β8|v1b-v2b|+β9|h1ζ-h2ζ|+β10|h1b-h2b|.

Theorem 3

The function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (q_1,q_2)$$\end{document}χ(q1,q2)defined by (3.1) satisfies all the axioms of metric (distance measure or distance function).

Proof

Wenyi et al.13 proved that the following conditions are satisfied for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1$$\end{document}χ1 : \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\ge \chi _1(q_1,q_2)\ge 0$$\end{document}1≥χ1(q1,q2)≥0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2)$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1=q_2$$\end{document}q1=q2,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2)=\chi _1(q_2,q_1)$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2)=χ1(q2,q1),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2)\le \chi _1(q_1,q_3)+\chi _1(q_3,q_2)$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2)≤χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q3,q2), consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ satisfies the metric axioms, and the proof of triangle inequality is given as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (q_1,q_2)&=\frac{\chi _1(q_1,q_2)}{1+\chi _1(q_1,q_2)}=\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\chi _1(q_1,q_2)}} \le \frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\chi _1(q_1,q_3)+\chi _1(q_3,q_2)}}\\ &=\frac{\chi _1(q_1,q_3)+\chi _1(q_3,q_2)}{1+\chi _1(q_1,q_3)+\chi _1(q_3,q_2)}\le \frac{\chi _1(q_1,q_3)+\chi _1(q_3,q_2)+2\chi _1(q_1,q_3)\chi _1(q_3,q_2)}{1+\chi _1(q_1,q_3)+\chi _1(q_3,q_2)+\chi _1(q_1,q_3)\chi _1(q_3,q_2)}\\ &=\frac{\chi _1(q_1,q_3)}{1+\chi _1(q_1,q_3)} +\frac{\chi _1(q_1,q_3)}{1+\chi _1(q_1,q_3)}=\chi (q_1,q_3)+\chi (q_3,q_2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(q1,q2)=χ1(q1,q2)1+χ1(q1,q2)=11+1χ1(q1,q2)≤11+1χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q3,q2)=χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q3,q2)1+χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q3,q2)≤χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q3,q2)+2χ1(q1,q3)χ1(q3,q2)1+χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q3,q2)+χ1(q1,q3)χ1(q3,q2)=χ1(q1,q3)1+χ1(q1,q3)+χ1(q1,q3)1+χ1(q1,q3)=χ(q1,q3)+χ(q3,q2),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (q_1,q_2)\le \chi (q_1,q_3)+\chi (q_3,q_2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(q1,q2)≤χ(q1,q3)+χ(q3,q2), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2) \ne 0$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2)≠0.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (q_1,q_2)$$\end{document}χ(q1,q2) is a distance function, we can prove the continuity of it. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}□

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\ge \chi _1(q_1,q_2)\ge 0$$\end{document}1≥χ1(q1,q2)≥0,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\ge \chi _1(q_1,q_2)\ge 0$$\end{document}1≥χ1(q1,q2)≥0,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2)$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1=q_2$$\end{document}q1=q2,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2)$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1=q_2$$\end{document}q1=q2,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1(q_1,q_2)=\chi _1(q_2,q_1)$$\end{document}χ1(q1,q2)=χ1(q2,q1),

Theorem 4

The function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_2)$$\end{document}χ(R1,R2)defined by (3.5) , (3.6) satisfy all the axioms of metric (distance measure or distance function).

Proof

The following conditions are satisfied for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1$$\end{document}χ1 : \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ satisfy the metric axioms, the proof of triangle inequality is given as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(R1,R2)=χ1(R1,R2)1+χ1(R1,R2)=11+1χ1(R1,R2)≤11+1χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)=χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)1+χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)≤χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)+2χ1(R1,R3)χ1(R3,R2)1+χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)+χ1(R1,R3)χ1(R3,R2)=χ1(R1,R3)1+χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R1,R3)1+χ1(R1,R3)=χ(R1,R3)+χ(R3,R2)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ satisfy the metric axioms, the proof of triangle inequality is given as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_2)&=\frac{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_2)}{1+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_2)} =\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_2)}}\le \frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3) +\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2)}}\\&=\frac{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2)}{1+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3) +\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2)}\le \frac{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2) +2\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2)}{1+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3) +\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2)+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)\chi _1(\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2)}\\&=\frac{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)}{1+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)} +\frac{\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)}{1+\chi _1(\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)} =\chi (\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)+\chi (\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(R1,R2)=χ1(R1,R2)1+χ1(R1,R2)=11+1χ1(R1,R2)≤11+1χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)=χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)1+χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)≤χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)+2χ1(R1,R3)χ1(R3,R2)1+χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R3,R2)+χ1(R1,R3)χ1(R3,R2)=χ1(R1,R3)1+χ1(R1,R3)+χ1(R1,R3)1+χ1(R1,R3)=χ(R1,R3)+χ(R3,R2)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_2)\le \chi (\textrm{R}_1,\textrm{R}_3)+\chi (\textrm{R}_3,\textrm{R}_2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(R1,R2)≤χ(R1,R3)+χ(R3,R2),

We observe that the distance measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ satisfies all the necessary conditions of a metric function, specifically non-negativity, identity of indiscernibles, symmetry, and triangular inequality. These conditions collectively establish that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is a valid metric. As a result, we can conclude that the properties of convergence and continuity, which are fundamental in metric spaces, hold for the proposed distance measure. This allows us to rigorously analyze the behavior of sequences of dual hesitant fuzzy sets (DHFS) under the distance measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ In the context of topology and analysis, this conclusion has far-reaching implications. The fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is a metric that implies that the set of all dual hesitant fuzzy sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{R}$$\end{document}R, equipped with the distance function given by equation (3.4), forms a well-defined metric space. This structure opens the door to various topological and analytical properties, such as the possibility of defining open and closed sets, compactness, and connectedness within the space of dual hesitant fuzzy sets. Furthermore, the establishment of a metric space enables the application of powerful theorems from analysis, such as the Banach fixed-point theorem, which could be used to explore fixed points and stability within this framework. Consequently, the formalization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{R}$$\end{document}R as a metric space provides a robust foundation for further exploration of dual hesitant fuzzy sets, not only in terms of practical applications like decision-making and medical diagnosis but also in theoretical research. The ability to discuss concepts such as limits, continuity, and convergence in this space allows for a deeper understanding of how dual hesitant fuzzy sets behave under transformations or aggregations, leading to new insights and methodologies in handling uncertain information in various fields.

In Fig. 1, the algorithm of the problem has been illustrated. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}□

Fig. 1Illustration of the algorithm through a chart.

Illustration of the algorithm through a chart.

Here is an example illustrating the use of dual hesitant fuzzy sets in medical diagnosis:

Here is an example illustrating the use of weighted dual hesitant fuzzy sets in decision-making:

Conclusion

We concluded from our findings that the distance measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ demonstrated superior performance in approximation, as evidenced by our comparative analysis. Our study encompassed various functions, including the variance function, upper bound function, average function, lower bound function, hesitancy degree of dual hesitant fuzzy elements, as well as score and accuracy functions, all of which were instrumental in the ranking of fuzzy sets. This capability was critical for effective decision-making and played a significant role in numerous topological and analytical studies of fuzzy sets. The lower and upper bound functions delineated the range of values for weighted dual hesitant fuzzy elements, while the mean function captured the collective opinion of decision-makers. The variance function quantified the dispersion of these opinions, and the hesitancy degree function encapsulated the uncertainty inherent in the decision-making process. Furthermore, the collection of dual hesitant fuzzy sets formed a metric space under the distance measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _1$$\end{document}χ1, thereby enabling the application of all associated properties of metric spaces. In our subsequent paper, we explored the application of these distance measures within the contexts of cluster analysis and image segmentation.
